### Question 101: 47,XYY Syndrome (Jacob Syndrome)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the cytogenetic basis (nondisjunction during meiosis), clinical features (tall stature, acne, normal fertility), and inheritance pattern of 47,XYY syndrome, differentiating it from other chromosomal abnormalities. (理解47,XYY综合征的细胞遗传学基础（减数分裂过程中的不分离）、临床特征（高身材、痤疮、生育能力正常）及其与其它染色体异常的鉴别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to the clinic due to concerns about his unusually tall stature (height 6'7") and severe nodulocystic acne that has persisted since adolescence, despite various dermatological treatments. He reports normal sexual development and fertility, having fathered two children with his wife. His medical history is unremarkable except for mild learning difficulties in childhood. His father was also notably tall (6'5"). Karyotype analysis is performed due to the constellation of symptoms. The karyotype reveals 47,XYY.

Which of the following mechanisms is the MOST likely cause of this patient's karyotype?

(A) Maternal nondisjunction during meiosis I resulting in an egg cell with two X chromosomes.
(B) Paternal nondisjunction during meiosis II resulting in a sperm cell with two Y chromosomes.
(C) Maternal nondisjunction during meiosis II resulting in an egg cell with two X chromosomes.
(D) Paternal nondisjunction during meiosis I resulting in a sperm cell with two X chromosomes.
(E) Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21, inherited from the father.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 35-year-old male, tall stature (6'7"), severe nodulocystic acne, normal fertility (fathered two children), mild childhood learning difficulties.
*   **Family History:** Father is also tall (6'5").
*   **Karyotype Result:** 47,XYY.
*   **Question:** Mechanism causing the 47,XYY karyotype.

The clinical features (tall stature, severe acne, normal fertility, mild learning difficulties) are characteristic of 47,XYY syndrome (Jacob syndrome). The karyotype confirms the presence of an extra Y chromosome. The question asks for the mechanism leading to this aneuploidy. 47,XYY arises from an error in meiosis, specifically nondisjunction, where homologous chromosomes or sister chromatids fail to separate properly. Since the patient is male (XY), the extra Y chromosome must have originated from either the sperm or the egg. Nondisjunction can occur during meiosis I or meiosis II.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **47,XYY Syndrome:** A sex chromosome aneuploidy where males have an extra Y chromosome.
*   **Clinical Features:** Often associated with tall stature, severe acne, learning disabilities (often mild), and normal fertility. Some individuals may have increased risk of certain health issues like cardiovascular problems or connective tissue disorders, but many are asymptomatic or have mild symptoms.
*   **Mechanism of Origin:** The most common cause is nondisjunction during spermatogenesis (meiosis in the father).
    *   **Meiosis I Nondisjunction:** Homologous chromosomes (XY) fail to separate. This results in sperm cells with either XXY or OY (empty). Fertilization of an OOY by a normal X-bearing sperm results in XO (Turner syndrome), and fertilization of an XXY by a normal X-bearing sperm results in XXX (Triple X syndrome). Fertilization of an XXY by a Y-bearing sperm results in XYY. Fertilization of an OY by a normal X-bearing sperm results in XY (normal).
    *   **Meiosis II Nondisjunction:** Sister chromatids fail to separate. In spermatogenesis, after meiosis I, the cells are XY. During meiosis II, the X and Y chromosomes should separate. If they don't, one sperm cell gets XY and the other gets OY. Fertilization of an OY by a normal X-bearing sperm results in XY (normal). Fertilization of an XY by a normal X-bearing sperm results in XXY (Klinefelter syndrome). Fertilization of an XY by a Y-bearing sperm results in XYY. Fertilization of an OY by a Y-bearing sperm results in YY (non-viable).
*   **Paternal vs. Maternal Origin:** While nondisjunction can occur in either parent, paternal origin (spermatogenesis) is more common for 47,XYY.
*   **Meiosis II vs. Meiosis I:** Meiosis II nondisjunction involving the Y chromosome is the most frequent cause of 47,XYY. This occurs when the Y chromosomes fail to separate during the second meiotic division in the father, producing a sperm cell with two Y chromosomes (YY). Fertilization of this sperm by a normal egg (X) results in the 47,XYY zygote.

Therefore, paternal nondisjunction during meiosis II, leading to a YY sperm, is the most likely mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Maternal nondisjunction during meiosis I resulting in an egg cell with two X chromosomes (XX). Fertilization by a normal Y-bearing sperm would result in XXY (Klinefelter syndrome), not 47,XYY. This is incorrect.
- (B): Paternal nondisjunction during meiosis II resulting in a sperm cell with two Y chromosomes (YY). Fertilization by a normal X-bearing egg would result in 47,XYY. This is the most common mechanism for 47,XYY syndrome. This is correct.
- (C): Maternal nondisjunction during meiosis II resulting in an egg cell with two X chromosomes (XX). Fertilization by a normal Y-bearing sperm would result in XXY (Klinefelter syndrome), not 47,XYY. This is incorrect.
- (D): Paternal nondisjunction during meiosis I resulting in a sperm cell with two X chromosomes (XX). This is impossible in a male as sperm carry either X or Y, not both X chromosomes simultaneously after meiosis I. Meiosis I separates homologous chromosomes (XY). Nondisjunction would lead to XXY or OY. Fertilization of XXY by X results in XXX, and fertilization of XXY by Y results in XYY. Fertilization of OY by X results in XO, and fertilization of OY by Y results in YY (non-viable). This option describes an impossible scenario in male meiosis. This is incorrect.
- (E): Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21, inherited from the father. This type of translocation is associated with Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) if the carrier parent contributes the translocated chromosome 21. It does not explain the presence of an extra Y chromosome (47,XYY). This is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
47,XYY syndrome results from nondisjunction during meiosis, most commonly paternal meiosis II, producing a YY sperm. Key features include tall stature, severe acne, and normal fertility. Differentiate this from Klinefelter syndrome (47,XXY, often associated with infertility due to testicular atrophy) and Turner syndrome (45,X, females, short stature, ovarian dysgenesis). Remember that sex chromosome aneuploidies often arise from meiotic errors.

### Question 102: 47,XXX Syndrome (Triple X)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the cytogenetic basis, clinical features, and inheritance pattern of aneuploidies, specifically 47,XXX syndrome resulting from maternal nondisjunction during oogenesis. (理解染色体数目异常的基础、临床特征和遗传模式，特别是由于卵母细胞减数分裂过程中母源性不分离导致的47,XXX综合征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to a genetics clinic for preconception counseling. She reports a history of mild developmental delay in childhood, including speech delay noted around age 3, and has always been taller than her peers (height 180 cm). Physical examination reveals epicanthal folds and slightly hypertelorism. Her mother is 58 years old and reports having conceived her at age 35. Karyotyping performed on the patient reveals 47,XXX. Her husband undergoes karyotyping, which shows a normal 46,XY. The patient is concerned about the risk of recurrence in future pregnancies.

(A) The risk of recurrence in future pregnancies is significantly increased due to the patient's 47,XXX karyotype being a balanced translocation.
(B) The risk of recurrence in future pregnancies is increased due to the patient's advanced maternal age at the time of her conception.
(C) The risk of recurrence in future pregnancies is low, as the 47,XXX karyotype arose from a sporadic maternal nondisjunction event during oogenesis, and the risk is primarily related to the mother's age at the time of conception.
(D) The risk of recurrence in future pregnancies is increased because the patient's father carries a balanced translocation involving the X chromosome.
(E) The risk of recurrence in future pregnancies is significantly increased because the patient's 47,XXX karyotype is heritable through her germline cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old female, tall stature (180 cm), mild developmental delay (speech delay), epicanthal folds, hypertelorism. These features are suggestive of 47,XXX syndrome (Triple X).
- **Karyotype:** Confirmed 47,XXX. This indicates an extra X chromosome in a female.
- **Family History:** Mother conceived at age 35. Advanced maternal age is a known risk factor for nondisjunction events.
- **Husband's Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY. This rules out paternal contribution to the aneuploidy.
- **Question:** Risk of recurrence in future pregnancies. This requires understanding the etiology of 47,XXX and its inheritance pattern.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **47,XXX Syndrome Etiology:** Triple X syndrome is typically caused by a random, sporadic error during meiosis, specifically nondisjunction of the X chromosomes in the mother's egg (oogenesis) or, less commonly, during the father's sperm formation (spermatogenesis). Nondisjunction means that homologous chromosomes or sister chromatids fail to separate properly.
- **Maternal Nondisjunction:** In this case, the mother conceived at age 35. Advanced maternal age is strongly correlated with an increased risk of nondisjunction events during oogenesis. The risk increases significantly after age 35.
- **Inheritance Pattern:** 47,XXX syndrome is not typically inherited. It arises from a de novo event (nondisjunction) in the germ cells of one of the parents. The patient herself has a 47,XXX karyotype, but this does not mean she will pass it on. Her own eggs will most likely have a normal number of chromosomes (46,XX) due to the random segregation of chromosomes during her own meiosis.
- **Recurrence Risk:** The recurrence risk for aneuploidies like 47,XXX is primarily determined by the risk of nondisjunction occurring again in the parent's germ cells. This risk is strongly influenced by maternal age at the time of conception. Since the event was likely sporadic and related to the mother's age at the time of the patient's conception, the risk in future pregnancies is low, although slightly elevated compared to younger mothers, and depends on the mother's age at the time of the next conception. It is not considered a heritable condition in the Mendelian sense.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** A balanced translocation involves rearrangements of chromosome segments without a net gain or loss of genetic material. While balanced translocations can lead to unbalanced gametes and aneuploidies in offspring, the patient's karyotype is 47,XXX, which is an aneuploidy (gain of a whole chromosome), not a translocation. Furthermore, the vignette does not provide any evidence of a translocation.
- (B): **Incorrect.** While the patient's mother's age (35) at the time of the patient's conception was a risk factor for the initial nondisjunction event that led to the patient's 47,XXX karyotype, it does not inherently increase the risk of recurrence in *future* pregnancies for the *patient* herself. The risk for the patient's future pregnancies depends on *her* age (or her partner's age if the event occurred during spermatogenesis, which is less common for X-chromosome aneuploidies) at the time of conception, not her mother's age at the time of her birth.
- (D): **Incorrect.** The husband's karyotype is normal (46,XY). A balanced translocation involving the X chromosome in the father could potentially lead to X-chromosome aneuploidies in daughters, but this is ruled out by the normal paternal karyotype.
- (E): **Incorrect.** 47,XXX syndrome is not typically heritable in the way that Mendelian disorders are. The patient's germline cells (eggs) will most likely undergo normal meiosis, producing eggs with 23 chromosomes (including one X chromosome). The aneuploidy arose from a meiotic error in the formation of the egg that was fertilized, not from a constitutional abnormality in the patient's somatic or germline cells that would be passed on directly. Therefore, the risk of recurrence is not significantly increased due to her own 47,XXX karyotype being heritable through her germline.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Takeaway:** Aneuploidies like 47,XXX typically arise from sporadic nondisjunction events during gametogenesis, often associated with advanced maternal age. They are generally not inherited, and the recurrence risk is primarily related to the age of the parent at the time of conception.
- **Differentials:** This question tests the understanding of the etiology and recurrence risk of chromosomal aneuploidies. Key differentials include understanding the difference between aneuploidy and translocation, the role of maternal age in nondisjunction, and the distinction between sporadic events and inherited chromosomal abnormalities. Other examples include Down syndrome (Trisomy 21), Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18), Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13), and Klinefelter syndrome (47,XXY).

### Question 103: Structural Chromosomal Rearrangements
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of structural chromosomal rearrangements, specifically reciprocal translocations, and their potential consequences, including the formation of fusion genes and their association with specific cancers. (考察结构染色体重排，特别是相互易位，及其后果，包括融合基因的形成及其与特定癌症的关联。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to his physician complaining of fatigue, bone pain, and frequent infections over the past 6 months. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he denies any family history of hematologic malignancies. Physical examination reveals pallor, splenomegaly, and multiple ecchymoses. Complete blood count (CBC) shows severe leukocytosis (WBC count 150,000/µL) with a predominance of immature granulocytes (blasts comprising 80% of WBCs), severe thrombocytopenia (platelet count 20,000/µL), and anemia (hemoglobin 7 g/dL). Bone marrow biopsy confirms the diagnosis of Chronic Myeloid Leukemia (CML). Cytogenetic analysis of the bone marrow cells reveals a shortened chromosome 22, designated as t(9;22)(q34;q11). Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) using probes for the ABL1 gene (located on chromosome 9q34) and the BCR gene (located on chromosome 22q11) confirms the presence of a fusion signal on the shortened chromosome 22, indicating the formation of the BCR-ABL1 fusion gene. The patient is started on imatinib, a tyrosine kinase inhibitor. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the oncogenic potential of the BCR-ABL1 fusion protein?

(A) The fusion protein acts as a dominant-negative inhibitor of normal ABL1 tyrosine kinase activity, leading to decreased cellular signaling.
(B) The fusion protein disrupts the normal function of the BCR protein, leading to loss of its GTPase activating protein (GAP) activity for Ras.
(C) The fusion protein exhibits constitutive tyrosine kinase activity due to the dysregulation of the ABL1 kinase domain, leading to uncontrolled cell proliferation and survival.
(D) The fusion protein causes chromosomal instability by interfering with DNA repair mechanisms, leading to increased mutation rates.
(E) The fusion protein acts as a transcription factor that represses the expression of tumor suppressor genes, leading to uncontrolled cell growth.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 45-year-old male with fatigue, bone pain, frequent infections, pallor, splenomegaly, ecchymoses. These symptoms suggest a hematologic malignancy affecting bone marrow function (anemia, thrombocytopenia) and immune system (infections).
- **CBC Results:** Severe leukocytosis with blast predominance (80%), severe thrombocytopenia, anemia. This is characteristic of acute leukemia or a blast crisis phase of a chronic leukemia.
- **Diagnosis:** Chronic Myeloid Leukemia (CML) confirmed by bone marrow biopsy.
- **Cytogenetics:** t(9;22)(q34;q11) - This is the Philadelphia chromosome, a hallmark of CML. It represents a reciprocal translocation between chromosomes 9 and 22.
- **FISH:** Confirms the fusion of ABL1 (9q34) and BCR (22q11) genes on the shortened chromosome 22, forming the BCR-ABL1 fusion gene.
- **Treatment:** Imatinib, a tyrosine kinase inhibitor, is used. This implies the target of imatinib is a tyrosine kinase.
- **Question:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the oncogenic potential of the BCR-ABL1 fusion protein.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Philadelphia Chromosome (t(9;22)):** This translocation creates a fusion gene, BCR-ABL1, on the derivative chromosome 22.
- **BCR-ABL1 Fusion Protein:** The BCR gene encodes a protein with multiple domains, including a GTPase activating protein (GAP) domain for Ras, a src homology 3 (SH3) domain, and a coiled-coil domain. The ABL1 gene encodes a non-receptor tyrosine kinase with SH2, SH3, and kinase domains.
- **Oncogenic Mechanism:** The fusion protein combines the N-terminal regulatory domains of BCR with the constitutively active tyrosine kinase domain of ABL1. The BCR portion, particularly the coiled-coil domain, disrupts the normal autoinhibitory mechanisms of the ABL1 kinase domain. This leads to the BCR-ABL1 fusion protein exhibiting *constitutive* (always "on") tyrosine kinase activity, independent of normal upstream signaling pathways.
- **Downstream Effects:** This unregulated tyrosine kinase activity phosphorylates numerous downstream targets involved in cell growth, proliferation, differentiation, adhesion, apoptosis, and genomic instability. Key targets include proteins in the Ras/MAPK pathway, PI3K/Akt pathway, and JAK/STAT pathway. This drives uncontrolled proliferation of myeloid cells, characteristic of CML.
- **Imatinib:** This drug specifically inhibits the ATP-binding site of the ABL1 kinase domain within the BCR-ABL1 fusion protein, blocking its tyrosine kinase activity and thus inhibiting the oncogenic signaling.
- **Therefore:** The BCR-ABL1 fusion protein's oncogenic potential stems from its *constitutive tyrosine kinase activity* due to the dysregulation of the ABL1 kinase domain by the BCR fusion partner.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** The BCR-ABL1 fusion protein is an *oncogene*, not a dominant-negative inhibitor. It *increases* tyrosine kinase activity, not decreases it. Normal ABL1 activity is tightly regulated, whereas BCR-ABL1 is constitutively active.
- (B): **Incorrect.** While the BCR protein normally functions as a GAP for Ras, the fusion protein's primary oncogenic mechanism is not the loss of this GAP activity. The constitutive kinase activity of the ABL1 domain is the main driver. Furthermore, the BCR portion in the fusion protein doesn't necessarily abolish its GAP function, and even if it did, this wouldn't be the primary mechanism of CML pathogenesis. The kinase activity is the key.
- (D): **Incorrect.** While uncontrolled cell proliferation can lead to genomic instability, the *primary* oncogenic mechanism of BCR-ABL1 is its constitutive tyrosine kinase activity, not direct interference with DNA repair. Genomic instability is a *consequence* of the dysregulated signaling, not the initial cause.
- (E): **Incorrect.** The BCR-ABL1 fusion protein is primarily a tyrosine kinase, not a transcription factor. Its oncogenic effect is mediated through phosphorylation of downstream signaling molecules, leading to altered gene expression and cell behavior, rather than directly repressing tumor suppressor genes as a transcription factor.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** The Philadelphia chromosome (t(9;22)) creates the BCR-ABL1 fusion gene, resulting in a constitutively active tyrosine kinase that drives CML. This is a classic example of an oncogenic fusion protein.
- **Differentials:** Other chromosomal translocations leading to fusion proteins include t(15;17) in Acute Promyelocytic Leukemia (APL), creating the PML-RARα fusion protein (which acts as an aberrant transcription factor), and t(11;14) in Mantle Cell Lymphoma, creating the Cyclin D1-IGH fusion protein (leading to overexpression of Cyclin D1). Understanding the specific function of the resulting fusion protein (kinase, transcription factor, etc.) is crucial.

---

<a id='question-104'></a>

### Question 104: Chromosomal Rearrangements
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating between balanced and unbalanced chromosomal rearrangements based on clinical presentation, karyotype analysis, and understanding the consequences of gain or loss of genetic material (遗传重排的平衡与不平衡：遗传物质的增失与表型).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to a genetics clinic after experiencing recurrent miscarriages. She and her husband have undergone extensive infertility workups, which have been largely unremarkable except for her abnormal karyotype. Her husband's karyotype is normal (46,XY). The patient's karyotype reveals a translocation between chromosomes 14 and 21, denoted as t(14;21)(q11;q10). She is phenotypically normal, with no dysmorphic features or developmental delays. Her husband is also phenotypically normal. A detailed analysis of the translocation breakpoint reveals that the translocation occurred within the non-coding regions of both chromosomes, specifically within the long arm (q) of chromosome 14 at band 11 and the long arm (q) of chromosome 21 at band 10. The patient's parents are deceased, and no family history of chromosomal abnormalities or developmental issues is available. Given the patient's karyotype and clinical presentation, what is the most likely explanation for her recurrent miscarriages?

(A) The translocation is unbalanced, leading to trisomy 21 in the patient's oocytes, causing developmental arrest in the resulting embryos.
(B) The translocation is balanced, but the patient's oocytes may receive either a normal chromosome 21, a normal chromosome 14, or the derivative chromosome 14;21, leading to aneuploidy in the resulting embryos.
(C) The translocation is balanced, and the patient's oocytes will always receive a normal chromosome 14 and a normal chromosome 21, ensuring euploidy in the resulting embryos.
(D) The translocation is unbalanced, resulting in a deletion of genetic material from chromosome 14, leading to haploinsufficiency of critical genes and causing recurrent miscarriages in the offspring.
(E) The translocation is balanced, but the patient's husband carries a reciprocal translocation t(14;21)(q10;q11), leading to unbalanced gametes and recurrent miscarriages.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old female, recurrent miscarriages, phenotypically normal. This suggests a carrier state for a genetic abnormality that affects offspring viability but doesn't cause severe issues in the carrier herself.
- **Karyotype:** t(14;21)(q11;q10). This indicates a Robertsonian translocation involving the long arms of chromosomes 14 and 21.
- **Breakpoint Location:** Within non-coding regions (q11 and q10). This is crucial because it suggests that the translocation itself might not involve the loss or gain of significant coding sequences, potentially making it balanced.
- **Husband's Karyotype:** Normal (46,XY). This rules out the husband being the source of unbalanced gametes.
- **Parental History:** Deceased, no family history. This means the translocation likely arose de novo in the patient or was inherited from a phenotypically normal parent who carried a balanced translocation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the explanation for recurrent miscarriages. This points to the effect of the translocation on gamete formation and subsequent embryonic development.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Chromosomal Translocation:** A rearrangement where a segment of one chromosome breaks off and attaches to another chromosome.
- **Balanced Translocation:** The total amount of genetic material remains the same. No significant loss or gain of genetic material occurs. Individuals with balanced translocations are typically phenotypically normal because they have the correct dosage of all essential genes. However, they can produce unbalanced gametes during meiosis.
- **Unbalanced Translocation:** There is a gain (duplication) or loss (deletion) of genetic material. Individuals with unbalanced translocations often have significant clinical phenotypes due to gene dosage abnormalities (trisomy or monosomy).
- **Meiosis in a Carrier of a Balanced Robertsonian Translocation:** During meiosis I, homologous chromosomes pair. The normal chromosome 14 pairs with the normal chromosome 21, and the derivative chromosome 14;21 pairs with its homolog (if any, which is usually absent in de novo cases). This pairing can lead to several possible outcomes for the resulting gametes:
    1. **Normal Gamete:** Receives a normal chromosome 14 and a normal chromosome 21.
    2. **Balanced Gamete:** Receives the derivative chromosome 14;21.
    3. **Unbalanced Gamete 1 (Trisomy 21):** Receives the derivative chromosome 14;21 *and* a normal chromosome 21 (gain of chromosome 21 material).
    4. **Unbalanced Gamete 2 (Trisomy 14):** Receives the derivative chromosome 14;21 *and* a normal chromosome 14 (gain of chromosome 14 material).
    5. **Unbalanced Gamete 3 (Monosomy 21):** Receives a normal chromosome 14 and *no* chromosome 21 (loss of chromosome 21 material).
    6. **Unbalanced Gamete 4 (Monosomy 14):** Receives a normal chromosome 21 and *no* chromosome 14 (loss of chromosome 14 material).
- **Consequences of Unbalanced Gametes:** Gametes with extra or missing chromosomes (aneuploidy) are often non-viable or lead to embryos with severe developmental abnormalities, resulting in miscarriage or stillbirth. Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) is a known cause of developmental issues, although the severity can vary. Monosomies for autosomes are typically lethal.
- **Patient's Phenotype:** The patient is phenotypically normal, strongly suggesting the translocation is balanced. The breakpoints are in non-coding regions, further supporting this.
- **Recurrent Miscarriages:** The production of unbalanced gametes (trisomy 14, trisomy 21, monosomy 14, monosomy 21) during oogenesis explains the recurrent miscarriages. The resulting embryos are likely aneuploid and non-viable.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. The patient is phenotypically normal, which is characteristic of a *balanced* translocation, not an unbalanced one. Unbalanced translocations typically cause significant phenotypes. While trisomy 21 can occur in offspring, the *patient* herself has a balanced translocation.
- (C): Incorrect. While the translocation is balanced *in the patient*, it does not guarantee that her oocytes will *always* receive normal chromosome complements. Meiotic segregation can lead to unbalanced gametes, as explained above. The statement that oocytes will *always* receive normal chromosomes is false.
- (D): Incorrect. The patient is phenotypically normal. An unbalanced translocation causing a deletion of genetic material from chromosome 14 would likely result in a phenotype in the patient due to haploinsufficiency. Furthermore, the breakpoints are described as being in non-coding regions, making a significant deletion less likely to cause a phenotype in the carrier.
- (E): Incorrect. The husband's karyotype is normal. He cannot be the carrier of a reciprocal translocation leading to unbalanced gametes in this scenario. The problem stems from the patient's translocation and its effect on her oocytes.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **Key Takeaway:** Balanced chromosomal rearrangements (like Robertsonian translocations involving non-coding regions) typically do not cause phenotypic abnormalities in the carrier but significantly increase the risk of producing unbalanced gametes, leading to aneuploidy in offspring and subsequent miscarriage or developmental abnormalities.
- **Differentials:** Consider other causes of recurrent miscarriages (e.g., uterine abnormalities, endocrine disorders, antiphospholipid syndrome, parental age, other genetic factors like uniparental disomy, microdeletions/duplications). However, given the specific karyotype finding, the translocation is the most direct explanation. Differentiate balanced vs. unbalanced translocations based on carrier phenotype and mechanism of gamete formation. Differentiate Robertsonian translocations (acrocentric chromosomes) from reciprocal translocations (affecting any chromosome).

---

<a id='question-105'></a>

### Question 105: Reciprocal Translocations: Exchange of Acentric Chromosome Segments Between Two Non-Homologous Chromosomes (e.g., t(9;22), t(8;14))
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Reciprocal translocations between non-homologous chromosomes can lead to the formation of fusion genes, often resulting in constitutive activation of oncogenes or disruption of tumor suppressor genes, driving tumorigenesis. (非同源染色体之间的相互易位可能导致融合基因的形成，通常会导致癌基因的持续激活或肿瘤抑制基因的破坏，从而驱动肿瘤发生。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of persistent fatigue, night sweats, and unintentional weight loss over the past 6 months. He has a history of smoking (20 pack-years) but no known family history of cancer. Physical examination reveals generalized lymphadenopathy, particularly in the cervical and axillary regions, and moderate splenomegaly. Complete blood count (CBC) shows marked leukocytosis (WBC 180,000/µL) with a predominance of mature-appearing lymphocytes. Cytogenetic analysis of bone marrow aspirate reveals a Philadelphia chromosome, characterized by a shortened chromosome 22 [t(9;22)(q34;q11.2)]. Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis confirms the presence of the BCR-ABL1 fusion gene. The patient is started on imatinib, a tyrosine kinase inhibitor. After 6 months, the patient experiences a complete hematologic remission, but molecular testing reveals the emergence of a new mutation in the ABL1 kinase domain (T315I). Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the resistance to imatinib observed in this patient?

(A) The T315I mutation causes a frameshift in the ABL1 gene, leading to premature termination of translation and loss of kinase activity.
(B) The T315I mutation alters the conformation of the BCR-ABL1 fusion protein, preventing its binding to the ATP-binding pocket of imatinib.
(C) The T315I mutation occurs within the ATP-binding pocket of the ABL1 kinase domain, sterically hindering imatinib binding while maintaining kinase activity.
(D) The T315I mutation leads to increased expression of the BCR-ABL1 fusion transcript, overwhelming the inhibitory capacity of imatinib.
(E) The T315I mutation results in the deletion of the BCR portion of the fusion gene, restoring normal ABL1 kinase function but making it insensitive to imatinib.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 45-year-old male with fatigue, night sweats, weight loss, lymphadenopathy, splenomegaly, and marked leukocytosis with mature lymphocytes – classic symptoms and signs of Chronic Myeloid Leukemia (CML).
- **Cytogenetics:** Philadelphia chromosome t(9;22)(q34;q11.2) – diagnostic hallmark of CML. This translocation fuses the ABL1 gene (tyrosine kinase) on chromosome 9 with the BCR gene on chromosome 22.
- **Molecular Genetics:** FISH confirms BCR-ABL1 fusion gene – confirms the molecular basis of the Philadelphia chromosome.
- **Treatment & Resistance:** Initial response to imatinib (a BCR-ABL1 tyrosine kinase inhibitor), followed by resistance due to a new ABL1 mutation (T315I).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism of imatinib resistance caused by the T315I mutation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **BCR-ABL1 Fusion Protein:** The t(9;22) translocation creates a fusion gene (BCR-ABL1) that encodes a constitutively active tyrosine kinase. The ABL1 kinase domain is responsible for the enzymatic activity.
- **Imatinib Mechanism:** Imatinib is a small molecule tyrosine kinase inhibitor specifically designed to bind to the ATP-binding pocket of the ABL1 kinase domain within the BCR-ABL1 fusion protein. By occupying this pocket, imatinib prevents ATP binding and subsequent phosphorylation of downstream substrates, thereby inhibiting the uncontrolled signaling that drives CML.
- **T315I Mutation:** This specific mutation (Threonine to Isoleucine at position 315) occurs within the ATP-binding pocket of the ABL1 kinase domain.
- **Resistance Mechanism:** The bulky isoleucine side chain at position 315 creates steric hindrance, physically preventing imatinib from binding effectively to the ATP-binding pocket. However, the kinase domain remains structurally intact and retains its enzymatic activity, allowing the BCR-ABL1 fusion protein to continue phosphorylating its substrates and driving CML progression. This is a classic example of a "gatekeeper" mutation conferring resistance to first-generation tyrosine kinase inhibitors like imatinib. Second- and third-generation inhibitors (e.g., dasatinib, nilotinib, bosutinib) were developed to overcome this resistance by binding to conformations of the kinase domain that are less affected by the T315I mutation or by binding more tightly.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** The T315I mutation is a point mutation (missense mutation) that substitutes one amino acid for another. It does *not* cause a frameshift. Frameshifts typically lead to premature stop codons and non-functional proteins, which would not explain the continued kinase activity and resistance.
- (B): **Incorrect.** While the mutation does alter the conformation of the protein, the primary mechanism of resistance is not simply a change in overall conformation preventing binding. The specific alteration is within the ATP-binding pocket, directly interfering with imatinib's access to its target site. The statement is too general and doesn't pinpoint the precise location and effect.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Increased expression of the fusion transcript might contribute to resistance in some contexts, but the primary mechanism of resistance to imatinib conferred by the T315I mutation is the direct alteration of the drug-binding site on the kinase protein itself, not increased protein levels. The mutation affects the protein's function (drug binding), not its quantity.
- (E): **Incorrect.** The T315I mutation occurs within the ABL1 portion of the fusion gene, specifically in the kinase domain. It does not involve deletion of the BCR portion. Furthermore, the mutation leads to a *constitutively active* kinase that is *resistant* to imatinib, not a restoration of normal ABL1 function.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Specific point mutations within the ATP-binding pocket of kinase domains (like T315I in ABL1) can confer resistance to targeted kinase inhibitors by sterically hindering drug binding while preserving kinase activity. This highlights the importance of understanding the precise molecular mechanism of drug resistance.
- **Differentials:** Other mechanisms of resistance to TKIs include amplification of the BCR-ABL1 gene, mutations outside the ATP-binding pocket that alter kinase conformation or substrate specificity, or activation of alternative signaling pathways bypassing BCR-ABL1. However, the T315I mutation is the most well-known example of resistance due to a specific mutation within the ATP-binding site.

---

<a id='question-106'></a>

### Question 106: Meiotic Segregation in Robertsonian Translocation Carriers
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanism of meiotic segregation in individuals carrying a Robertsonian translocation, specifically the difference between alternate and adjacent segregation patterns and their consequences on gamete viability and offspring phenotype. (理解罗伯逊易位携带者减数分裂过程中的分离模式，特别是交替分离和相邻分离模式的差异及其对配子活力和后代表型的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 42-year-old woman presents to a genetic counseling clinic with her husband. They have been trying to conceive for two years without success. She reports having had three prior miscarriages, all occurring in the first trimester. Her husband is phenotypically normal. Karyotyping reveals that the woman is a carrier of a balanced Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21, denoted as t(14;21)(q10;q10). Her husband's karyotype is normal (46,XY). The couple undergoes preimplantation genetic diagnosis (PGD) on embryos obtained via in vitro fertilization (IVF). Analysis of the embryos reveals the following chromosomal complements:
- 3 embryos: 46,XX,der(14;21)(q10;q10) (Balanced translocation carrier, phenotypically normal)
- 2 embryos: 47,XX,+14,+21 (Trisomy 14 and Trisomy 21)
- 1 embryo: 45,X, monosomy for an autosome (not specified which one)
- 4 embryos: 46,XY,der(14;21)(q10;q10) (Balanced translocation carrier, phenotypically normal)
- 1 embryo: 47,XY,+14,+21 (Trisomy 14 and Trisomy 21)
- 1 embryo: 45,Y, monosomy for an autosome (not specified which one)
- 2 embryos: 46,XX (Normal)
- 2 embryos: 46,XY (Normal)

Which meiotic segregation pattern is most likely responsible for the production of the embryos with trisomy 14 and trisomy 21?

(A) Alternate segregation of the normal chromosomes 14 and 21 and the translocated chromosome 14;21.
(B) Adjacent-1 segregation of the normal chromosomes 14 and 21 and the translocated chromosome 14;21.
(C) Adjacent-2 segregation of the normal chromosomes 14 and 21 and the translocated chromosome 14;21.
(D) Non-disjunction of chromosome 14 during meiosis I in the female.
(E) Non-disjunction of chromosome 21 during meiosis II in the female.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** A woman with recurrent miscarriages and infertility is found to have a balanced Robertsonian translocation t(14;21). Her husband is normal.
- **Karyotype:** The woman is t(14;21)(q10;q10), meaning the long arms of chromosomes 14 and 21 are fused, forming a single derivative chromosome, der(14;21). She has two copies of the derivative chromosome and no normal copies of chromosomes 14 or 21.
- **PGD Results:** The embryos show a range of chromosomal complements, including balanced carriers (der(14;21)/der(14;21)), trisomies (both +14 and +21), monosomies (autosomal), and normal diploidy.
- **Question:** Asks for the meiotic segregation pattern leading to the trisomy 14 and trisomy 21 embryos.
- **Key Clue:** The presence of embryos with *both* chromosome 14 and chromosome 21 in addition to the derivative chromosome indicates that the normal chromosome 14 and the normal chromosome 21 were inherited together from the mother. This specific combination points to a particular type of adjacent segregation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Robertsonian Translocation:** Involves fusion of two acrocentric chromosomes (chromosomes with the centromere near one end) at the centromere. In t(14;21), the long arms (q arms) of chromosomes 14 and 21 fuse. The short arms (p arms) are typically lost.
- **Meiosis in Carrier:** The carrier has two copies of the der(14;21) chromosome. During meiosis, these chromosomes must pair. Quadrivalent pairing occurs, where the two der(14;21) chromosomes pair with the two normal chromosomes 14 and 21 (which are present in very low numbers, often absent, in the female carrier due to loss during meiosis or non-viability).
- **Segregation Patterns:** Several segregation patterns are possible during meiosis II (assuming normal meiosis I):
    - **Alternate Segregation:** One der(14;21) goes to one pole, and the other der(14;21) goes to the other pole. This results in gametes that are either normal (no chromosome 14 or 21) or balanced carriers (der(14;21)). This pattern produces viable, phenotypically normal offspring or balanced carriers.
    - **Adjacent-1 Segregation:** One der(14;21) segregates with the normal chromosome 14, and the other der(14;21) segregates with the normal chromosome 21. This results in gametes with der(14;21) + 14 and der(14;21) + 21. Fertilization with a normal gamete from the father produces offspring with trisomy 14 and trisomy 21 (e.g., 47,XX,+14,+21). These trisomies are typically lethal early in development, explaining the miscarriages.
    - **Adjacent-2 Segregation:** Both der(14;21) chromosomes segregate together to one pole, and the normal chromosomes 14 and 21 segregate together to the other pole. This results in gametes with der(14;21) + der(14;21) (balanced carrier) and 14 + 21 (nullisomy for 14 and 21, usually lethal).
    - **3:1 Segregation:** Three chromosomes go to one pole and one to the other. This can result in gametes with der(14;21) + 14 + 21 (nullisomy for der(14;21)) or der(14;21) + 14 or der(14;21) + 21 (monosomy for the other chromosome), which are typically non-viable.
- **Analysis of PGD Results:** The presence of embryos with +14 and +21 confirms that Adjacent-1 segregation occurred. The normal embryos (46,XX/XY) and balanced carrier embryos (der(14;21)/der(14;21)) are consistent with alternate and adjacent-2 segregation patterns. The monosomic embryos are consistent with 3:1 segregation or adjacent-2 segregation (nullisomy for der(14;21)).
- **Conclusion:** The specific combination of trisomy 14 and trisomy 21 is the hallmark of Adjacent-1 segregation in a Robertsonian translocation carrier.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Alternate segregation leads to gametes with either der(14;21) (balanced carrier) or no chromosome 14/21 (normal). Fertilization produces normal offspring or balanced carriers. This does *not* produce trisomy 14 and trisomy 21.
- (B): Adjacent-1 segregation leads to gametes with der(14;21) + 14 and der(14;21) + 21. Fertilization with a normal gamete produces offspring with trisomy 14 and trisomy 21. This matches the observed PGD results (embryos with +14 and +21). This is the correct mechanism.
- (C): Adjacent-2 segregation leads to gametes with der(14;21) + der(14;21) (balanced carrier) and 14 + 21 (nullisomy for 14 and 21). Fertilization produces balanced carriers or offspring with nullisomy for 14 and 21, which are typically lethal. This does *not* produce trisomy 14 and trisomy 21.
- (D): Non-disjunction of chromosome 14 during meiosis I in the female would lead to gametes with two copies of chromosome 14 (or none). Fertilization would result in trisomy 14 (or monosomy 14), not the combination of trisomy 14 *and* trisomy 21 seen in the PGD results. This mechanism doesn't account for the inheritance of both chromosome 14 and chromosome 21 together.
- (E): Non-disjunction of chromosome 21 during meiosis II in the female would lead to gametes with two copies of chromosome 21 (or none). Fertilization would result in trisomy 21 (or monosomy 21), not the combination of trisomy 14 *and* trisomy 21 seen in the PGD results. This mechanism doesn't account for the inheritance of both chromosome 14 and chromosome 21 together.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Robertsonian translocations (like t(14;21)) lead to quadrivalent formation during meiosis. Different segregation patterns (alternate, adjacent-1, adjacent-2) result in different gamete types and offspring phenotypes, including balanced carriers, trisomies (often lethal), and nullisomies (lethal). Adjacent-1 segregation specifically produces gametes carrying both a normal chromosome and the derivative chromosome, leading to offspring with trisomy for both involved chromosomes.
- **Differentials:** Understand the difference between Robertsonian translocations (fusion of acrocentric chromosomes) and reciprocal translocations. Recognize that Robertsonian translocations often involve loss of short arms, which usually don't carry many genes, making the carrier phenotypically normal. Differentiate between the segregation patterns in Robertsonian translocations and the consequences of non-disjunction events (e.g., Down syndrome due to trisomy 21 from non-disjunction).

---

<a id='question-107'></a>

### Question 107: Robertsonian Translocation
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Robertsonian translocation involves the fusion of the long arms of two acrocentric chromosomes, leading to a decrease in chromosome number but potentially causing genetic imbalance and associated phenotypes, particularly when involving chromosome 21. (Robertsonian易位涉及两个近端着丝粒染色体的长臂融合，导致染色体数目减少，但可能引起遗传失衡和相关表型，特别是当涉及21号染色体时。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old woman presents to a genetics clinic after experiencing recurrent miscarriages. She and her husband have undergone extensive infertility workups, which have been largely unremarkable except for her karyotype. Her karyotype analysis reveals 45 chromosomes, including a large metacentric chromosome. Her husband's karyotype is normal (46,XY). She reports no significant personal medical history or family history of genetic disorders, although her mother had a history of intellectual disability. Physical examination is unremarkable. A detailed analysis of the large metacentric chromosome using G-banding and fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) probes confirms that it is derived from the fusion of the long arms of chromosomes 14 and 21, with loss of the short arms (p arms). The patient is currently pregnant, and prenatal testing reveals the fetus has a karyotype of 46,XY,der(14;21)(q10;q10),+21. Given this information, what is the most likely clinical presentation of the fetus?

(A) Cri-du-chat syndrome, characterized by microcephaly, intellectual disability, and a high-pitched cat-like cry.
(B) Down syndrome (Trisomy 21), characterized by intellectual disability, characteristic facial features, and congenital heart defects.
(C) Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13), characterized by severe intellectual disability, microcephaly, cleft lip/palate, and polydactyly.
(D) Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18), characterized by severe intellectual disability, micrognathia, clenched hands with overlapping fingers, and rocker-bottom feet.
(E) Turner syndrome (45,X), characterized by short stature, ovarian dysgenesis, and webbed neck.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old woman with recurrent miscarriages, normal infertility workup except for karyotype.
- **Patient Karyotype:** 45 chromosomes, including a large metacentric chromosome. This indicates a chromosomal abnormality involving fusion.
- **Cytogenetic Analysis:** G-banding and FISH confirm the large metacentric chromosome is a Robertsonian translocation between chromosomes 14 and 21, with loss of short arms (der(14;21)(q10;q10)). This means the long arms of chromosomes 14 and 21 have fused.
- **Husband's Karyotype:** Normal (46,XY).
- **Fetal Karyotype:** 46,XY,der(14;21)(q10;q10),+21. This indicates the fetus inherited the Robertsonian translocation chromosome (der(14;21)) from the mother and also has an extra copy of chromosome 21 (likely from a non-disjunction event during meiosis in the mother, as she carries the balanced translocation).
- **Question:** Predict the most likely clinical presentation of the fetus.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Robertsonian Translocation:** This is a type of chromosomal rearrangement where the long arms of two acrocentric chromosomes (chromosomes with the centromere near one end, i.e., 13, 14, 15, 21, 22) fuse at the centromere, forming a single metacentric chromosome. The short arms (p arms), which contain ribosomal RNA genes and are generally less important genetically, are lost.
- **Balanced vs. Unbalanced Translocation:** The mother has a *balanced* Robertsonian translocation, meaning she has the correct total amount of genetic material (45 chromosomes, but the material is rearranged). She is usually phenotypically normal but has a high risk of producing gametes with an *unbalanced* number of chromosomes.
- **Meiosis in Carrier:** During meiosis, the chromosomes involved in the translocation (14 and 21 in this case) must pair properly. This can lead to several types of gametes:
    1.  Normal gamete (one normal 14, one normal 21)
    2.  Translocation gamete (der(14;21))
    3.  Nullisomic gamete (no 14, no 21)
    4.  Duplication gamete (two normal 14s, two normal 21s)
- **Fetal Karyotype Interpretation:** The fetus has 46 chromosomes, including the der(14;21) chromosome inherited from the mother. The "+21" indicates an additional free chromosome 21. This means the fetus inherited a translocation chromosome (der(14;21)) from the mother and an extra chromosome 21 from either the mother (via a duplication gamete) or the father (via a normal gamete, but this is less likely given the mother's translocation). The net result is three copies of the long arm of chromosome 21 (two free copies and one copy within the translocation chromosome).
- **Clinical Consequence:** Having three copies of the long arm of chromosome 21 results in Trisomy 21, also known as Down syndrome. This condition is characterized by intellectual disability, distinctive facial features (e.g., upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, flat nasal bridge), congenital heart defects (e.g., AV septal defect), hypotonia, and increased risk of certain medical conditions (e.g., leukemia, Alzheimer's disease).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Cri-du-chat syndrome** is caused by a deletion on the short arm of chromosome 5 (5p-). The characteristic cry is due to abnormalities in the larynx and nervous system. This is unrelated to a Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21.
- (C): **Patau syndrome** is caused by Trisomy 13 (an extra copy of chromosome 13). It is characterized by severe congenital anomalies incompatible with long-term survival. While Robertsonian translocations can involve chromosome 13, the fetus in this case has an extra chromosome 21, not 13.
- (D): **Edwards syndrome** is caused by Trisomy 18 (an extra copy of chromosome 18). It is also characterized by severe congenital anomalies and poor prognosis. Again, the fetus has an extra chromosome 21, not 18.
- (E): **Turner syndrome** is caused by monosomy X (45,X) or structural abnormalities of the X chromosome. It affects females and is characterized by short stature, ovarian failure, and specific physical features. This is unrelated to the Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Robertsonian translocations involving chromosome 21 significantly increase the risk of having offspring with Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) due to unbalanced segregation during meiosis. Understanding the mechanism of Robertsonian translocation, balanced vs. unbalanced karyotypes, and the resulting aneuploidies is crucial. Differentiate this from other chromosomal abnormalities like deletions (Cri-du-chat), and other trisomies (Patau, Edwards).

<a id='question-108'></a>

### Question 108: Robertsonian Translocation Carrier
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the clinical presentation and cytogenetic findings in carriers of balanced Robertsonian translocations, specifically distinguishing them from individuals with unbalanced translocations or other chromosomal abnormalities. (理解罗伯逊易位携带者的临床表现和细胞遗传学发现，特别是将其与具有不平衡易位或其他染色体异常的个体区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old woman, G2P1, presents for routine prenatal screening. She reports no significant past medical history and denies any known family history of genetic disorders or intellectual disability. Her first pregnancy resulted in the birth of a healthy child. During this pregnancy, routine amniocentesis was performed at 16 weeks gestation due to her age (32 years). Karyotype analysis of the amniotic fluid cells reveals a karyotype of 45,XX,rob(14;21)(q10;q10). The patient is phenotypically normal, with no dysmorphic features, normal intelligence, and normal physical examination findings. Her husband undergoes karyotyping, which shows a normal male karyotype (46,XY). The couple is concerned about the risk of having a child with Down syndrome.

(A) The fetus has a 50% chance of inheriting an unbalanced translocation resulting in Down syndrome due to the mother's carrier status.
(B) The mother's karyotype indicates she is a carrier of a balanced translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21, which carries a risk for future offspring.
(C) The mother's karyotype indicates she is a carrier of a balanced translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21, and the risk of having a child with Down syndrome is significantly higher than the general population risk based on maternal age alone.
(D) The mother's karyotype indicates she is a carrier of an unbalanced translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21, explaining her normal phenotype despite having only 45 chromosomes.
(E) The mother's karyotype indicates she is a carrier of a balanced translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21, but the risk of having a child with Down syndrome is negligible because she is phenotypically normal.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old woman, G2P1, routine prenatal screening. Age is relevant for baseline Down syndrome risk.
- **Family History:** No known family history of genetic disorders or intellectual disability. This makes a *de novo* mutation or a previously unknown carrier status more likely.
- **Prenatal Screening:** Amniocentesis performed.
- **Karyotype Result:** 45,XX,rob(14;21)(q10;q10). This is the crucial piece of information.
    - 45 chromosomes: Indicates a loss of one chromosome, but the presence of rob(14;21) explains this.
    - XX: Normal female sex chromosomes.
    - rob(14;21): Indicates a Robertsonian translocation involving the long arms (q) of chromosomes 14 and 21 at band q10. Robertsonian translocations involve acrocentric chromosomes (13, 14, 15, 21, 22) and result in the loss of the short arms (p arms), which contain non-essential ribosomal RNA genes. The fusion of the long arms creates a single, larger chromosome.
    - The notation 45,XX,rob(14;21)(q10;q10) means the individual has 45 chromosomes, including the fused chromosome derived from 14 and 21.
- **Patient Phenotype:** Phenotypically normal, normal intelligence, normal physical exam. This indicates the translocation is balanced in the mother.
- **Husband's Karyotype:** Normal (46,XY). This is important for calculating the risk to the offspring.
- **Question Stem:** Concerned about the risk of having a child with Down syndrome. This requires understanding the implications of the mother's carrier status.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Robertsonian Translocations:** These are structural chromosomal rearrangements involving the fusion of two acrocentric chromosomes at their centromeres. The short arms (p arms) are typically lost. Chromosomes 14 and 21 are common participants.
- **Balanced vs. Unbalanced:**
    - **Balanced:** The individual has the correct total amount of genetic material, although rearranged. Carriers are usually phenotypically normal. The mother in the vignette has a balanced translocation: she has lost the short arms of chromosomes 14 and 21, but gained the long arms fused together. Her total genetic material is essentially normal (44 chromosomes + the fused chromosome = 45 chromosomes, but the *content* is balanced).
    - **Unbalanced:** The individual has an abnormal amount of genetic material (either extra or missing). This usually leads to developmental abnormalities, intellectual disability, and specific syndromes.
- **Mechanism of Risk:** A carrier of a balanced Robertsonian translocation (like the mother) can produce gametes with unbalanced chromosome complements. This occurs during meiosis I when homologous chromosomes pair and exchange genetic material.
    - **Mother's Meiosis:** The mother has one normal chromosome 14, one normal chromosome 21, and one fused chromosome 14;21.
    - **Possible Gametes:**
        1. Normal chromosome 14 + Normal chromosome 21 (Normal)
        2. Normal chromosome 14 + Fused chromosome 14;21 (Balanced carrier, like mother)
        3. Fused chromosome 14;21 + Normal chromosome 21 (Unbalanced: Trisomy 21 - Down syndrome)
        4. Fused chromosome 14;21 + Normal chromosome 14 (Unbalanced: Monosomy 14 - usually lethal)
    - **Probability:** Each of these gametes has roughly a 25% chance of being produced (assuming normal segregation).
- **Risk to Offspring:**
    - 25% chance of a normal karyotype (inheriting a normal 14 and a normal 21).
    - 25% chance of being a balanced carrier (inheriting the fused chromosome 14;21).
    - 25% chance of having Down syndrome (Trisomy 21, inheriting the fused chromosome 14;21 and a normal 21).
    - 25% chance of having Monosomy 14 (inheriting the fused chromosome 14;21 and a normal 14). Monosomy 14 is typically lethal, resulting in miscarriage.
- **Overall Risk:** The risk of having a child with Down syndrome due to the mother's translocation is approximately 25% (from the Trisomy 21 gamete). This risk is *in addition* to the age-related risk of Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) in the general population. The mother's age (32) does confer a baseline risk, but the translocation significantly increases the risk.
- **Husband's Role:** The husband has a normal karyotype, so he contributes either a normal chromosome 14 or a normal chromosome 21 to the offspring. He cannot contribute the unbalanced chromosome complement.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** The risk is not 50%. The probability of producing a gamete with an unbalanced translocation (Trisomy 21) is 25%, not 50%. This option confuses the probability of the mother passing on the *translocation chromosome* (which is 50%, as she has one normal 14, one normal 21, and one fused 14;21) with the probability of producing an *unbalanced* gamete leading to Down syndrome (which is 25%).
- (B): **Incorrect.** While the mother *is* a carrier of a balanced translocation, this statement alone is incomplete. It doesn't address the *risk* to future offspring, which is the core of the question. The question asks about the risk of Down syndrome, which is a direct consequence of her carrier status.
- (D): **Incorrect.** The mother's karyotype indicates a *balanced* translocation, not an unbalanced one. Unbalanced translocations typically cause significant phenotypic abnormalities. The mother is phenotypically normal, confirming the translocation is balanced. Also, having 45 chromosomes is characteristic of Robertsonian translocations, but it doesn't inherently mean the translocation is unbalanced; the *balance* refers to the genetic content.
- (E): **Incorrect.** The risk of having a child with Down syndrome is *not* negligible. As calculated above, the risk due to the translocation is approximately 25%, which is significantly higher than the baseline risk for a 32-year-old woman (around 1 in 850). The fact that the mother is phenotypically normal indicates the translocation is balanced, but it does not eliminate the risk to her offspring.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Carriers of balanced Robertsonian translocations (especially involving chromosome 21) have a significantly increased risk (approx. 25%) of having offspring with Down syndrome due to the production of unbalanced gametes. The carrier themselves is usually phenotypically normal.
- **Differentials:**
    - **Unbalanced Robertsonian Translocation:** Causes Down syndrome (if involving chromosome 21) or other syndromes (e.g., Monosomy 13/14) with associated phenotypic abnormalities.
    - **Trisomy 21 (Down Syndrome):** Caused by an extra copy of chromosome 21 (usually due to nondisjunction), not a translocation. Karyotype would be 47,XX,+21 or 47,XY,+21.
    - **Mosaic Down Syndrome:** Presence of both normal and Trisomy 21 cells. Karyotype would show two or more cell lines.
    - **Other Chromosomal Abnormalities:** Deletions, duplications, inversions, etc., can also cause genetic disorders, but the specific karyotype 45,XX,rob(14;21) points directly to a balanced Robertsonian translocation carrier.

<a id='question-109'></a>

### Question 109: Translocation Down Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular basis and inheritance patterns of chromosomal abnormalities, specifically Robertsonian translocations involving chromosome 21, leading to an increased risk of Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) in offspring. (理解染色体异常的分子基础和遗传模式，特别是涉及21号染色体的罗伯逊易位，导致后代患21体综合征（唐氏综合征）的风险增加。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old woman, G2P1, presents for prenatal counseling. Her first pregnancy resulted in the birth of a healthy son. However, her younger brother was diagnosed with Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) at birth and died in infancy due to congenital heart defects. Family history is otherwise unremarkable. Karyotyping performed on the patient reveals a balanced Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21, denoted as t(14;21)(q10;q10). The patient and her husband desire another child. Genetic counseling indicates a significantly increased risk of having a child with Down syndrome. Which of the following mechanisms best explains the increased risk of Trisomy 21 in the offspring of this carrier mother?

(A) Nondisjunction of chromosome 14 during maternal meiosis I, leading to a gamete with two copies of chromosome 14 and one copy of chromosome 21, which upon fertilization with a normal gamete results in Trisomy 14.
(B) Nondisjunction of chromosome 21 during maternal meiosis II, leading to a gamete with two copies of chromosome 21, which upon fertilization with a normal gamete results in Trisomy 21.
(C) Nondisjunction of the rearranged chromosome 14;21 during maternal meiosis I, leading to a gamete with two copies of the rearranged chromosome 14;21, which upon fertilization with a normal gamete results in Trisomy 21.
(D) Nondisjunction of the rearranged chromosome 14;21 during maternal meiosis II, leading to a gamete with two copies of the rearranged chromosome 14;21, which upon fertilization with a normal gamete results in Trisomy 21.
(E) Nondisjunction of chromosome 21 during paternal meiosis I, leading to a gamete with two copies of chromosome 21, which upon fertilization with a normal gamete results in Trisomy 21.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old woman, G2P1, seeking prenatal counseling.
*   **Family History:** Brother with Down syndrome (Trisomy 21). This suggests a potential genetic basis for Trisomy 21 in the family.
*   **Karyotype:** Balanced Robertsonian translocation t(14;21)(q10;q10). This is the critical piece of information. A balanced translocation means the patient has the correct total amount of genetic material, but it's rearranged. The long arms (q) of chromosomes 14 and 21 are fused at the centromere.
*   **Genetic Counseling:** Increased risk of Trisomy 21 in offspring. This confirms the significance of the translocation.
*   **Question:** Mechanism explaining the increased risk.

The key is understanding how a *balanced* translocation in a parent leads to an *unbalanced* translocation (Trisomy 21) in the offspring. The translocation involves chromosomes 14 and 21. The patient has one normal copy of chromosome 14, one normal copy of chromosome 21, and one fused chromosome 14;21.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Robertsonian Translocation:** This type of translocation involves the fusion of two acrocentric chromosomes (chromosomes with the centromere near one end, like 13, 14, 15, 21, 22) at their centromeres. The short arms (p) are typically lost, containing non-essential genes (like rRNA genes, which are present on other chromosomes).
*   **Balanced Carrier:** The patient has the karyotype 45,XX,t(14;21)(q10;q10). She has one normal chromosome 14, one normal chromosome 21, and one fused chromosome 14;21. Her total chromosome number is 45, but her total genetic material is normal (two copies of chromosome 14 material and two copies of chromosome 21 material). She is phenotypically normal.
*   **Meiosis:** During gamete formation (meiosis), the chromosomes must pair and segregate properly. The patient's chromosomes 14 and 21, along with the fused chromosome 14;21, must form a trivalent structure (a group of three chromosomes).
*   **Possible Segregation Patterns:**
    *   **Alternate Segregation (Meiosis I):** The normal chromosome 14 goes to one daughter cell, and the normal chromosome 21 and the fused chromosome 14;21 go to the other daughter cell. This results in gametes with:
        *   Normal chromosome 14, normal chromosome 21 (normal)
        *   Normal chromosome 14, fused chromosome 14;21 (unbalanced - effectively Trisomy 21, as the fused chromosome carries both 14 and 21 material)
    *   **Adjacent-1 Segregation (Meiosis I):** The normal chromosome 14 and the fused chromosome 14;21 go to one daughter cell, and the normal chromosome 21 goes to the other daughter cell. This results in gametes with:
        *   Normal chromosome 14, fused chromosome 14;21 (unbalanced - effectively Trisomy 21)
        *   Normal chromosome 21 (normal)
    *   **Adjacent-2 Segregation (Meiosis I):** The normal chromosome 21 and the fused chromosome 14;21 go to one daughter cell, and the normal chromosome 14 goes to the other daughter cell. This results in gametes with:
        *   Normal chromosome 21, fused chromosome 14;21 (unbalanced - effectively Trisomy 21)
        *   Normal chromosome 14 (normal)
    *   **Nondisjunction:** Errors in segregation can also occur. Nondisjunction means a chromosome pair fails to separate properly during meiosis I or meiosis II.
*   **Risk Calculation:** The risk of producing an unbalanced gamete (carrying extra chromosome 21 material) depends on the specific segregation pattern. For a t(14;21) carrier, the empirical risk of having a child with Trisomy 21 is approximately 10-15%. This risk arises from the various segregation errors during meiosis.
*   **Analyzing the Options:**
    *   **(D) Nondisjunction of the rearranged chromosome 14;21 during maternal meiosis II:** If the fused chromosome 14;21 fails to divide properly in meiosis II, one gamete will receive two copies of the fused chromosome (14;21)(14;21), and the other will receive none. Fertilization with a normal gamete will result in a zygote with the karyotype 47,XX,+14,+21 (or 47,XY,+14,+21), which is Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome). This is a plausible mechanism contributing to the increased risk.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Nondisjunction of chromosome 14 during maternal meiosis I, leading to a gamete with two copies of chromosome 14 and one copy of chromosome 21, which upon fertilization with a normal gamete results in Trisomy 14.** This describes nondisjunction of a *normal* chromosome 14. While nondisjunction can occur, it would lead to Trisomy 14, not Trisomy 21. The patient has a balanced translocation, and the risk is specifically for Trisomy 21 due to the involvement of chromosome 21 in the translocation.
*   **(B) Nondisjunction of chromosome 21 during maternal meiosis II, leading to a gamete with two copies of chromosome 21, which upon fertilization with a normal gamete results in Trisomy 21.** This describes the most common mechanism for *sporadic* Trisomy 21 (nondisjunction of the normal chromosome 21), but it doesn't account for the specific translocation present in the mother. While maternal age increases the risk of nondisjunction of chromosome 21, the primary mechanism increasing risk in *this specific patient* is related to the translocation.
*   **(C) Nondisjunction of the rearranged chromosome 14;21 during maternal meiosis I, leading to a gamete with two copies of the rearranged chromosome 14;21, which upon fertilization with a normal gamete results in Trisomy 21.** This describes nondisjunction of the fused chromosome during meiosis I. This is a valid mechanism that can lead to Trisomy 21. However, nondisjunction during meiosis II (Option D) is also a significant contributor and is often considered a more frequent cause of unbalanced gametes in translocation carriers compared to meiosis I errors. The question asks for *a* mechanism, and both C and D are possible. However, the empirical risk (~10-15%) is often attributed more heavily to errors in segregation of the trivalent during meiosis II. Meiosis II nondisjunction events are generally more frequent than Meiosis I events for Robertsonian translocations.
*   **(E) Nondisjunction of chromosome 21 during paternal meiosis I, leading to a gamete with two copies of chromosome 21, which upon fertilization with a normal gamete results in Trisomy 21.** This describes nondisjunction of the normal chromosome 21 in the *father*. The father is not the carrier of the translocation. The risk is increased because the *mother* is the carrier.

**Comparison of C and D:** Both Meiosis I and Meiosis II nondisjunction of the rearranged chromosome can lead to Trisomy 21. However, studies suggest that errors in segregation during Meiosis II are more common than those during Meiosis I for Robertsonian translocations. Therefore, Meiosis II nondisjunction (Option D) is often cited as the primary mechanism contributing to the elevated risk in carriers.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
*   **High-Yield Takeaway:** Balanced Robertsonian translocations involving chromosome 21 (e.g., t(14;21)) significantly increase the risk of having a child with Trisomy 21 due to errors in chromosome segregation during meiosis (nondisjunction or alternate/adjacent segregation) leading to unbalanced gametes. The risk is typically 10-15%.
*   **Differentials:** Understand the difference between balanced and unbalanced translocations. Differentiate the mechanisms of Trisomy 21 caused by translocation carriers versus sporadic Trisomy 21 (maternal age-related nondisjunction). Recognize that Robertsonian
