<a id='question-149'></a>

### Question 149: Translocation Down Syndrome: rob(14;21) Carrier Having High Theoretical Risk of Conceiving Trisomy 21 (Empirical Risk ~10-15% if Mother is Carrier) - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the mechanism of unbalanced chromosomal segregation during meiosis in a Robertsonian translocation carrier, specifically a t(14;21) carrier, leading to a high risk of Trisomy 21 in offspring. (理解罗伯逊易位携带者，特别是t(14;21)携带者，在减数分裂过程中发生染色体分离失衡的机制，导致后代患21三体综合征的高风险。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old woman, G2P1, presents for prenatal counseling. Her first pregnancy resulted in the birth of a healthy child. However, her younger brother has Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) due to a standard, free trisomy 21. Family history is otherwise unremarkable. Karyotyping performed on the patient reveals a balanced Robertsonian translocation, t(14;21)(q10;q10). She is currently 10 weeks pregnant. The patient expresses significant anxiety about the risk of her current fetus having Down syndrome. She asks you to explain the specific risks associated with her translocation and the potential outcomes for her offspring.

(A) The risk of the fetus having Trisomy 21 is approximately 1%, similar to the general population risk, because the translocation is balanced in the mother.
(B) The risk of the fetus having Trisomy 21 is significantly increased, primarily due to nondisjunction events during oogenesis, resulting in gametes with either two copies of chromosome 21 or none. The empirical risk is approximately 10-15%.
(C) The risk of the fetus having Trisomy 21 is significantly increased, primarily due to nondisjunction events during spermatogenesis in the father (assuming the father is the source of the translocation), resulting in gametes with either two copies of chromosome 21 or none. The empirical risk is approximately 10-15%.
(D) The risk of the fetus having Trisomy 21 is significantly increased, primarily due to the formation of a tetrasomic gamete (containing two copies of both chromosome 14 and chromosome 21) during meiosis, which then fuses with a normal gamete. The empirical risk is approximately 10-15%.
(E) The risk of the fetus having Trisomy 21 is significantly increased, primarily due to the formation of a nullisomic gamete (lacking both chromosome 14 and chromosome 21) during meiosis, which then fuses with a normal gamete. The empirical risk is approximately 10-15%.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old woman, G2P1, seeking prenatal counseling. Her brother has Down syndrome (Trisomy 21).
- **Key Finding:** Patient karyotype shows a balanced Robertsonian translocation t(14;21)(q10;q10).
- **Question:** What is the risk to the fetus of having Trisomy 21, and what is the underlying mechanism?
- **Clue 1:** The patient has a *balanced* translocation. This means she has the correct amount of genetic material but it's rearranged. She is phenotypically normal.
- **Clue 2:** Her brother has *standard* Trisomy 21 (free trisomy 21), indicating the translocation is not the cause of his condition.
- **Clue 3:** The translocation involves chromosomes 14 and 21. Robertsonian translocations involve the fusion of two acrocentric chromosomes (chromosomes 13, 14, 15, 21, 22) at the centromere.
- **Clue 4:** The question asks for the risk to the *fetus*. This implies the patient is the carrier and is producing the gametes.
- **Clue 5:** The empirical risk for a t(14;21) carrier mother is known to be elevated, around 10-15%.
- **Deduction:** A balanced translocation carrier produces gametes through meiosis. Due to the fused chromosome 14 and 21, segregation can occur in several ways:
    1.  Normal (chromosome 14 + chromosome 21)
    2.  Trisomy 21 (translocated chromosome 14;21 + normal chromosome 21)
    3.  Trisomy 14 (translocated chromosome 14;21 + normal chromosome 14)
    4.  Monosomy 14 (none)
    5.  Monosomy 21 (none)
    6.  Nullisomy 14;21 (none)
    The most common unbalanced gametes are Trisomy 21 and Trisomy 14. Trisomy 21 gametes, when fertilized by a normal sperm, result in Trisomy 21 offspring. Trisomy 14 gametes, when fertilized by a normal sperm, result in Trisomy 14 offspring (which is usually lethal). Nullisomic gametes are also possible but less frequent. Nondisjunction events (failure of chromosomes to separate properly) during meiosis are the underlying cause of these unbalanced gametes. Since the mother is the source of the gametes in this scenario, the risk is due to nondisjunction during oogenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Robertsonian Translocation:** A specific type of chromosomal rearrangement where two acrocentric chromosomes fuse at their centromeres, losing the short arms (which contain non-essential ribosomal RNA genes). The long arms fuse to form a single, larger chromosome. In t(14;21), the long arms of chromosomes 14 and 21 fuse.
- **Balanced vs. Unbalanced Carrier:** The mother has a *balanced* translocation, meaning she has 45 chromosomes but all the necessary genetic material. She is phenotypically normal. However, during meiosis, the paired translocated chromosome (14;21) and its normal homologues (14 and 21) form a trivalent structure.
- **Meiotic Segregation:** The segregation of this trivalent is complex and prone to errors. Possible outcomes include:
    - **Alternate Segregation:** The translocated chromosome goes to one pole, and the two normal chromosomes go to the other pole. This produces balanced gametes (n+1 chromosome 14;21) and normal gametes (n).
    - **Adjacent-1 Segregation:** The translocated chromosome goes to one pole, and one normal chromosome (e.g., chromosome 21) goes to the other pole. This produces unbalanced gametes: a gamete with two copies of chromosome 21 (Trisomy 21) and a gamete with no chromosome 21 (Nullisomy 21).
    - **Adjacent-2 Segregation:** The translocated chromosome goes to one pole, and the other normal chromosome (e.g., chromosome 14) goes to the other pole. This produces unbalanced gametes: a gamete with two copies of chromosome 14 (Trisomy 14) and a gamete with no chromosome 14 (Nullisomy 14).
    - **3:1 Segregation:** All three chromosomes (translocated and two normal) go to one pole, and the other pole gets nothing. This produces unbalanced gametes: a gamete with three copies of chromosome 14 and three copies of chromosome 21 (Tetrasomy 14;21) and a gamete with no chromosome 14 or 21 (Nullisomy 14;21).
- **Risk Calculation:** The most common unbalanced gametes produced by a t(14;21) carrier are Trisomy 21 and Trisomy 14. Nullisomic gametes are also produced. Fertilization of these unbalanced gametes leads to offspring with Trisomy 21, Trisomy 14, or monosomies. Trisomy 14 and monosomies are generally non-viable. Therefore, the primary risk is Trisomy 21. The empirical risk for a t(14;21) carrier mother is approximately 10-15%, significantly higher than the age-related risk in the general population. This elevated risk is due to the inherent segregation errors (nondisjunction-like events) during meiosis involving the translocated chromosomes.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. The risk is *not* 1% (general population risk). The balanced translocation significantly increases the risk due to abnormal segregation during meiosis. The mother is a carrier, and her gametes can lead to Trisomy 21 in the offspring.
- (C): Incorrect. While nondisjunction is the underlying mechanism, the question specifies the *patient* is the carrier (mother). Therefore, the risk arises from errors during *oogenesis* (egg formation), not spermatogenesis (sperm formation). The father's contribution is typically assumed to be normal unless stated otherwise.
- (D): Incorrect. While tetrasomic gametes (containing two copies of both chromosome 14 and 21) can theoretically form, they are much less common than Trisomy 21 gametes resulting from adjacent segregation. The primary mechanism leading to the high empirical risk is the production of Trisomy 21 gametes.
- (E): Incorrect. While nullisomic gametes (lacking both chromosome 14 and 21) can form, they are also less common than Trisomy 21 gametes. Furthermore, fertilization of a nullisomic gamete would lead to monosomy 14 and monosomy 21, which are typically non-viable. The main risk is Trisomy 21 resulting from gametes containing two copies of chromosome 21.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- High-Yield Takeaway: Balanced Robertsonian translocations, particularly t(14;21), significantly increase the risk of Trisomy 21 in offspring due to errors in meiotic segregation (nondisjunction-like events) leading to unbalanced gametes. The empirical risk is ~10-15% for a carrier mother.
- Differentials: Standard Trisomy 21 (free trisomy 21) vs. Translocation Trisomy 21. Mosaicism. Other Robertsonian translocations (e.g., t(13;14), t(21;22)). Nondisjunction leading to standard trisomy 21 (age-related risk).

<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Robertsonian Translocation (rob(21;21))
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Robertsonian translocation involving chromosome 21 (rob(21;21)) leads to a carrier phenotype but results in 100% risk of offspring having trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) or spontaneous abortion due to unbalanced gametes. (罗伯逊易位涉及21号染色体 (rob(21;21)) 会导致携带者表型正常，但由于不平衡配子，后代有100%风险患有21三体综合征（唐氏综合征）或流产。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old woman, gravida 2, para 1, presents for prenatal counseling. Her first pregnancy resulted in the birth of a healthy child. However, her husband has a known history of Down syndrome (Trisomy 21). Karyotyping performed on the husband confirms a Robertsonian translocation involving two copies of chromosome 21, written as rob(21;21)(q10;q10). The woman undergoes prenatal diagnostic testing via chorionic villus sampling (CVS) at 10 weeks gestation. The CVS karyotype reveals 46,XY,der(21;21)(q10;q10)+21. Based on this information, what is the most likely outcome for this pregnancy?

(A) The fetus will be phenotypically normal with a balanced karyotype.
(B) The fetus will have Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) due to an extra copy of the long arm of chromosome 21.
(C) The fetus will have mosaicism for Trisomy 21, with some cells having a normal karyotype and others having 47 chromosomes.
(D) The fetus will have a balanced translocation karyotype, similar to the husband, but without the extra chromosome 21 material.
(E) The fetus will have a partial monosomy for the short arm of chromosome 21, leading to a distinct phenotype different from Down syndrome.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient History:** 32-year-old woman, G2P1, husband has Down syndrome. This immediately raises suspicion for a chromosomal abnormality related to chromosome 21.
*   **Husband's Karyotype:** rob(21;21)(q10;q10). This is a Robertsonian translocation involving two copies of chromosome 21. The breakpoints are at the q10 bands, which are the centromeric regions. This means the long arms (q arms) of the two chromosome 21s fuse, and the short arms (p arms) are lost. The husband is phenotypically normal because he has the correct total chromosome number (45) and the genetic material from the long arms of chromosome 21 is present, albeit rearranged.
*   **Fetal Karyotype:** 46,XY,der(21;21)(q10;q10)+21. This indicates the fetus has 46 chromosomes, is male (XY), has a derivative chromosome formed by the fusion of the long arms of chromosome 21 (der(21;21)(q10;q10)), and *also* has an extra, separate copy of chromosome 21 (+21). This means the fetus has three copies of the long arm of chromosome 21, resulting in Trisomy 21.
*   **Question:** What is the most likely outcome for this pregnancy? This asks for the consequence of the fetal karyotype.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Robertsonian Translocation (rob(21;21)):** This specific type of translocation involves the fusion of the long arms of two acrocentric chromosomes (chromosomes with the centromere near one end, like 13, 14, 15, 21, 22) at the centromere, with loss of the short arms. In rob(21;21), the long arms of two chromosome 21s fuse.
*   **Carrier Phenotype:** Individuals with a balanced Robertsonian translocation (like the husband) usually have a normal phenotype because they possess the correct amount of genetic material, although it is rearranged. The loss of the short arms (p arms) of chromosome 21 usually involves non-essential genes (like rRNA genes), so it doesn't cause significant phenotypic effects.
*   **Meiosis and Gamete Formation:** During meiosis in the husband (rob(21;21) carrier), chromosome pairing can occur in several ways:
    *   **Adjacent-1:** The two normal chromosome 21s pair with each other, and the translocation chromosome pairs with itself. This results in gametes with:
        *   Normal chromosome 21 (n=2)
        *   Translocation chromosome (n=1)
    *   **Adjacent-2:** One normal chromosome 21 pairs with one copy of the translocation chromosome, and the other normal chromosome 21 pairs with the other copy of the translocation chromosome. This results in gametes with:
        *   Normal chromosome 21 (n=2)
        *   Translocation chromosome (n=1)
        *   **Nullisomy 21 (n=0)** - *This is the critical point.*
        *   **Trisomy 21 (n=3)** - *This is the critical point.*
    *   **3:21:** All three chromosome 21s (two normal and the translocation) pair together. This results in gametes with:
        *   Normal chromosome 21 (n=2)
        *   **Nullisomy 21 (n=0)**
        *   **Trisomy 21 (n=3)**
*   **Fetal Karyotype Interpretation:** The fetus has 46 chromosomes, including the derivative chromosome der(21;21) and an additional, separate chromosome 21. This means the fetus has *three* copies of the long arm of chromosome 21, which contains the critical region for Down syndrome (DSCR - Down Syndrome Critical Region on 21q22.3). This constitutes Trisomy 21.
*   **Outcome:** Trisomy 21 is associated with Down syndrome. The risk of having a child with Down syndrome when one parent is a carrier of rob(21;21) is approximately 100% for surviving offspring, as all possible gametes from the carrier parent will lead to either nullisomy 21 (which is lethal, resulting in spontaneous abortion) or trisomy 21.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): The fetus will be phenotypically normal with a balanced karyotype. **Incorrect.** The fetal karyotype is 46,XY,der(21;21)(q10;q10)+21, which is *unbalanced*. It has three copies of the long arm of chromosome 21, leading to Trisomy 21, not a balanced karyotype. A balanced karyotype would involve no net gain or loss of genetic material (e.g., the husband's karyotype is balanced).
- (C): The fetus will have mosaicism for Trisomy 21, with some cells having a normal karyotype and others having 47 chromosomes. **Incorrect.** Mosaicism occurs when there are two or more cell lines with different genotypes in the same individual. The CVS sample represents the genetic makeup of the placental tissue, which is indicative of the fetus's overall genetic constitution. The karyotype provided (46,XY,der(21;21)(q10;q10)+21) describes a single, consistent chromosomal complement for all cells analyzed in the sample, not mosaicism.
- (D): The fetus will have a balanced translocation karyotype, similar to the husband, but without the extra chromosome 21 material. **Incorrect.** The husband has a balanced translocation (rob(21;21)). The fetus has an *unbalanced* karyotype (46,XY,der(21;21)(q10;q10)+21) because it has an extra copy of chromosome 21 material. The fetus does *not* have a balanced translocation.
- (E): The fetus will have a partial monosomy for the short arm of chromosome 21, leading to a distinct phenotype different from Down syndrome. **Incorrect.** The husband, being a carrier of rob(21;21), loses the short arms (p arms) of both chromosome 21s. While this loss occurs, the short arms contain genes that are not essential for survival or major phenotype (like rRNA genes). The critical genetic material for Down syndrome resides on the long arm (q arm). The fetus has *extra* material from the long arm (+21), not a monosomy for the short arm. The phenotype will be Down syndrome due to the trisomy of the long arm material.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Robertsonian translocations involving chromosome 21 (rob(21;21)) in a carrier parent lead to a 100% risk of unbalanced gametes resulting in either nullisomy 21 (lethal) or trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) in offspring. This is a high-yield concept for understanding chromosomal abnormalities and their inheritance patterns. Differential diagnoses include other Robertsonian translocations (e.g., rob(14;21)), reciprocal translocations involving chromosome 21, and nondisjunction leading to Trisomy 21. However, rob(21;21) specifically carries the 100% risk due to the nature of the translocation and gamete formation.

---
